MICROCONTROLLERS (IS42)

Microcontrollers & Embedded Systems

Welcome to the premium student study portal. This dashboard compiles exhaustive class notes, solved university questions (CIE & SEE), and standard assembly & C laboratory scripts to prepare you to ace the exams.

CIE & SEE PYQs (2023-2025)

Course Syllabus Blueprint

Unit 1: ARM Embedded Systems & SoC
RISC vs CISC rules, ARM design parameters, System-on-Chip (SoC) blocks, timers, watchdogs, PLL clock multipliers, and PWM duty cycle mechanics.
Unit 2: Peripherals, Memory & Software
Serial protocols (I2C, SPI, UART), Direct Memory Access (DMA), SRAM asynchronous read/write cycles, Thermal Design Power, endianness, and CPU status flags.
Unit 3: ARM7 Architecture & Instruction Set
ARM7 operating modes, register bank mapping, exceptions/vectors sequences, 3-stage pipeline operations, shifted operands barrel shifting, and branching.
Unit 4: Memory Access & Interfacing
LDR/STR indexing (pre/post-indexing with write-back), block memory LDM/STM stacks, LPC2148 microchip bus topology, timer register sets, and Embedded C pin control.
Unit 5: ARM Cortex-M0/M0+ NVIC
Cortex-M0 core architecture, Thread/Handler modes, Special registers (MSP/PSP/CONTROL), memory map specifications, NVIC nesting states, and low-power modes.

Slide 4 PPT 1: Introduction to Embedded Systems
ARM Embedded Systems
  • ARM(Advanced
  • RISC Machine) is a 32-bit architecture microcontroller that was developed by Acorn Computers in 1983.
  • ARM

    • is basically a family of Reduced Instruction Set Computing (RISC) architecture-based microprocessors. ARM microcontrollers consist of ARM processors, RAM, ROM, and I/O peripherals. ARM microcontrollers are used in a wide range of applications due to their low power consumption, low cost, and high performance.

  • One

    • of the important features of ARM microcontrollers is that they are highly customizable depending on requirements of the applications. Therefore, it is highly versatile microcontroller architecture.

Slide 5 PPT 1: Introduction to Embedded Systems
ARM Embedded Systems
  • We

    • can use assembly language as well as high level programming languages such as C, C++ to program the ARM microcontrollers. ARM microcontrollers are highly scalable;

  • Some popular microcontrollers of ARM family are ARM Cortex-M0 to M7, LPC2148, etc
  • .
  • The
  • communication protocols used in ARM microcontrollers are UART, USART, SPI, I2C, I2S, LIN, CAN, DSP, SAI, and IrDA.
  • ARM microcontrollers have faster clock speed, allowing them to process more instructions per second.
Slide 6 PPT 1: Introduction to Embedded Systems
The RISC design philosophy
  • The ARM core uses a RISC architecture.

    • RISC is a design philosophy aimed at delivering simple but powerful instructions that execute within a single cycle at a high clock speed.

    The RISC philosophy concentrates on reducing the complexity of instructions performed by the hardware because it is easier to provide greater flexibility and intelligence in software rather than hardware.

  • As a result, a RISC design places greater demands on the compiler
  • .
Slide 7 PPT 1: Introduction to Embedded Systems
In contrast, the traditional complex instruction set computer (CISC) relies more on the hardware for instruction functionality, and consequently the CISC instructions are more complicated.
Slide 8 PPT 1: Introduction to Embedded Systems
Differences between RISC and CISC
  • Sl
  • No
  • CISC
  • RISC
  • 1
  • CISC is the short form for Complex Instruction Set Computer.
  • RISC refers to Reduced Instruction Set Computer.
  • 2
  • Instruction format is variable
  • Instruction format is Fixed
  • 3

    • The CISC architecture processes complex instructions that require several clock cycles for execution. On average, it takes two to five clock cycles per instruction (CPI).

    The RISC architecture executes simple yet optimized instructions in a single clock cycle. It processes instructions at an average speed of 1.5 clock cycles per instruction (CPI).

  • 4
  • CISC focuses on hardware, such as transistors, to execute instructions.
  • RISC focuses more on software such as codes or compilers to execute instructions.
  • 5
  • CISC uses a variety of instructions to accomplish complex tasks.
  • RISC is provided with a reduced instruction set, which is typically primitive in nature.
  • 6
  • A CISC processor works with 16 bits to 64 bits to execute each instruction.
  • A RISC processor utilizes 32 bits to execute each instruction.
  • 7

    • A CISC architecture uses one cache to store data as well as instructions. However, recent CISC designs employ split caches to divide data and instructions.

  • The RISC architecture relies on split caches, one for data and the other for instructions.
  • 8
  • CISC processors use a memory-to-memory framework to execute instructions such as ADD, LOAD, and even STORE.
  • RISC processors rely on a register-to-register mechanism to execute ADD, STORE, and independent LOAD instructions.
Slide 9 PPT 1: Introduction to Embedded Systems
Differences between RISC and
  • CISC contd..
  • 9
  • The CISC architecture uses only one register set.
  • The RISC architecture utilizes multiple register sets.
  • 10
  • Since CISC devices operate in a multi-clock environment, it supports addressing modes in the range of 12 to 24.
  • Since RISC machines operate on single clock cycles, it has limited addressing modes.
  • 11
  • CISC processors are capable of processing high-level programming language statements more efficiently

    • Since RISC processors support a limited set of addressing modes, complex instructions are synthesized through software codes.

  • 12
  • CISC does not support parallelism and pipelining. As such, CISC instructions are less pipelined.
  • RISC processors support instruction pipelining.
  • 13
  • CISC instructions require high execution time.
  • RISC instructions require less time for execution.
  • 14

    • Some examples of CISC processors include Intel x86 CPUs, Motorola 68000 family,AMD(Advanced Micro Devices), 8086 family, 8051.

    Examples of RISC processors include ARM(Advance Risc machine), MIPS(Microprocessor without Interlocked Pipeline Stages), SPARC(Scalable Processor Architecture), Power, RISC-V

  • 15
  • CISC processors are used for low-end applications such as home automation devices, security systems, etc.
  • RISC processors are suitable for high-end applications, including image and video processing, telecommunications, etc.
  • 16
  • ADD AX, BX
  • ADD R3, R1, R2
  • 17
  • MOV AX, A ; Load A into AX
  • ADD AX, B ; AX = AX + B (memory operand)
  • MOV C, AX ; Store result into C
  • LDR R1, A ; Load A into register R1
  • LDR R2, B ; Load B into register R2
  • ADD R3, R1, R2 ; R3 = R1 + R2
  • STR R3, C ; Store result into C
Slide 10 PPT 1: Introduction to Embedded Systems
RISC and CISC Major Differences
Slide 11 PPT 1: Introduction to Embedded Systems
The
  • RISC philosophy is implemented with four major design rules:
  • Instructions
  • —
  • RISC
  • processors have a reduced number of instruction classes.
  • These
  • classes provide simple operations that can each execute in a single cycle
  • .

    • The compiler or programmer synthesizes complicated operations (for example, a divide operation) by combining several simple instructions

  • .

    • Each instruction is a fixed length to allow the pipeline to fetch future instructions before decoding the current instruction

  • .
  • In contrast, in CISC processors the instructions are often of variable size and take many cycles to execute.
Slide 12 PPT 1: Introduction to Embedded Systems
2.
  • Pipelines
  • —
  • The
  • processing of instructions is broken down into smaller units that can be
  • executed in parallel by pipelines
  • .
  • Ideally the pipeline advances by one step on each cycle for maximum throughput.
  • Instructions
  • can be decoded in one pipeline stage
  • .
  • There is no need for an instruction to be executed by a mini-program called microcode as on CISC processors.
  • 3.
Slide 13 PPT 1: Introduction to Embedded Systems
3.
  • Registers
  • —
  • RISC
  • machines have a large general-purpose register set
  • .

    • Any register can contain either data or an address. Registers act as the fast local memory store for all data processing operations.

  • In
  • contrast, CISC processors have dedicated registers for specific purposes.
Slide 14 PPT 1: Introduction to Embedded Systems
4.
  • Load-store
  • architecture:
  • The
  • processor operates on data held in registers.
  • Separate
  • load
  • and store instructions transfer data between the register bank and external memory
  • .

    • Memory accesses are costly, so separating memory accesses from data processing provides an advantage because you can use data items held in the register bank multiple times without needing multiple memory accesses

  • .
  • In contrast, with a CISC design the data processing operations can act on memory directly
  • .
Slide 15 PPT 1: Introduction to Embedded Systems
These design rules allow a RISC processor to be simpler, and thus the core can operate at higher clock frequencies
  • .
  • In contrast, traditional CISC processors are more complex and operate at lower clock frequencies.
Slide 16 PPT 1: Introduction to Embedded Systems
The ARM Design Philosophy
  • There are a number of physical features that have driven the ARM (Advanced RISC Machines) processor design.
  • First, portable embedded systems require some form of battery power.
  • The ARM processor has been specifically designed to be small to reduce power consumption and extend battery
  • operation—essential
  • for applications such as mobile
  • phones
  • and personal digital assistants (PDAs).
Slide 17 PPT 1: Introduction to Embedded Systems
The ARM Design Philosophy
  • High

    • code density is another major requirement since embedded systems have limited memory due to cost and/or physical size restrictions.

    High code density is useful for applications that have limited on-board memory, such as mobile phones and mass storage devices.

    ARM has incorporated hardware debug technology within the processor so that software engineers can view what is happening while the processor is executing code.

Slide 18 PPT 1: Introduction to Embedded Systems
With

greater visibility, software engineers can resolve issues faster, which has a direct effect on the time to market and reduces overall development costs

  • .
  • The ARM core is not a pure RISC architecture because of the constraints of its primary application—the embedded system
  • .
  • The strength of the ARM core is that it does not take the RISC concept too far.
  • In today’s systems the key is not raw processor speed but total effective system performance and power consumption.
    • Slide 19 PPT 1: Introduction to Embedded Systems
    Instruction Set for Embedded Systems
    • The

      • ARM instruction set differs from the pure RISC definition in several ways that make the ARM instruction set suitable for embedded applications

    • :
    Slide 20 PPT 1: Introduction to Embedded Systems
    Variable
    • cycle execution for certain instructions
    • —
    • Not
    • every ARM instruction executes
    • in a single cycle.
    • For

      • example, load-store-multiple instructions vary in the number of execution cycles depending upon the number of registers being transferred

    • .

      • The transfer can occur on sequential memory addresses, which increases performance since sequential memory accesses are often faster than random accesses.

    • Code
    • density is also improved since multiple register transfers are common operations at the start and end of functions.
    Slide 21 PPT 1: Introduction to Embedded Systems
    Inline barrel shifter leading to more complex instructions—
    • The inline barrel shifter is

      • a hardware component that pre-processes one of the input registers before it is used by an instruction. This expands the capability of many instructions to improve core performance and code density.

    • Thumb 16-bit instruction set—
    • ARM enhanced the processor core by adding a second

      • 16-bit instruction set called Thumb that permits the ARM core to execute either 16- or 32-bit instructions. The 16-bit instructions improve code density by about 30% over 32-bit fixed-length instructions.

    Slide 22 PPT 1: Introduction to Embedded Systems
    The Thumb instruction set is a subset of the most commonly used 32-bit ARM instructions.

    16-bit Thumb is a set of instructions in computer programming that are half the length of standard ARM instructions. They are used to reduce the size and cost of code storage memory in ARM processors.

    Thumb instructions are each 16 bits long, and have a corresponding 32-bit ARM instruction that has the same effect on the processor model.

  • Thumb
  • 16-bit:
    • Slide 23 PPT 1: Introduction to Embedded Systems
    Thumb instructions operate with the standard ARM register configuration, allowing excellent interoperability between ARM and Thumb states.
    • Thumb

      • , therefore, makes the ARM7TDMI core ideally suited to embedded applications with restricted memory bandwidth, where code density is important

    • .

      • The availability of both 16-bit Thumb and 32-bit ARM instruction sets gives designers the flexibility to emphasize performance or code size on a subroutine level, according to the requirements of their applications.

    Slide 24 PPT 1: Introduction to Embedded Systems
    For

    example, critical loops for applications such as fast interrupts and DSP algorithms can be coded using the full ARM instruction set then linked with Thumb code

  • .
  • Thumb-2:

    • Thumb-2 technology, introduced in ARMv6T2, extended the original Thumb instruction set with 32-bit instructions, enabling it to achieve performance similar to ARM code while maintaining improved code density.

    Slide 25 PPT 1: Introduction to Embedded Systems
    Conditional execution—
    • An instruction is only executed when a specific condition has
    • been satisfied. This feature improves performance and code density by reducing branch instructions.
    • Enhanced instructions—
    • The enhanced digital signal processor (DSP) instructions were

      • added to the standard ARM instruction set to support fast 16×16-bit multiplier operations and saturation. These instructions allow a faster-performing ARM processor in some cases to replace the traditional combinations of a processor plus a DSP.

    • These additional features have made the ARM processor one of the most commonly used 32-bit embedded processor cores.

    Each system
    • is meant for a particular task or set of tasks. This task set is not alterable later.

      • Any electronic device of now has a processing unit which takes in sensor data, process it and then produces signals, which does some actuation. The “processing” is usually done by software.

    • An

      • embedded system has an electronics hardware with software embedded into it. This makes the processing unit to be a microprocessor.

      To get the sensor data, input peripherals are needed, and for output actuation, output peripherals are needed. Adding such peripherals to a microprocessor, makes the processing unit to become a Microcontroller unit (MCU). We also use as

    • SoC
    • (System on Chip).
    • Characteristics of embedded systems

    • systems

      Many of them have to operate and produce results within a stipulated time. Thus, we say that some of them need to do time critical computations.

      Application wise- the domain is very fast. Some of them are placed in inaccessible areas, such as forests, some in extreme climatic conditions like

    • in case
    • of military and space applications. Some will be very simple, as Temperature sensors, pedometers etc..

    • refers to the maximum amount of power (in watts) that a device or component can safely dissipate as heat without being
    • damaged.
    • The size of the
    • device.
    • The memory ( RAM and ROM) capacity of the MCU.

    Some embedded applications are time critical, such as ABS (
    • Anti-lock Braking
    • System) in cars.

    • If not, we say that the system failure has occurred.
    • In order to manage that, it needs RTOS (Real Time Operating System) which imposes and ensures the timing criterion.
    • An RTOS uses deadline-based task scheduling algorithm.
    • So, most of the embedded systems around us is managed and controlled by RTOS.

    Slide 17 PPT 3: SoC Infrastructure & Reset
    03-03-2026
    • Feature
    • Power-On Reset (POR)
    • Brown-Out Reset (BOR)
    • Watchdog Reset (WDT)
    • Trigger Condition
    • When power is first applied
    • When supply voltage drops below safe level
    • When software fails to refresh watchdog timer
    • Cause
    • Power startup
    • Voltage dip (brown-out)
    • Program hang / infinite loop
    • Voltage Condition
    • 0V → Normal Vcc
    • Normal Vcc → Below threshold
    • Voltage is normal
    • Purpose
    • Initialize system at startup
    • Protect during unstable/low voltage
    • Recover from software crash
    • Hardware/Software
    • Hardware-based
    • Hardware-based
    • Hardware timer monitored by software
    • When it Happens
    • Once at power-up
    • Any time voltage drops
    • Any time program stops responding
    • Used For
    • Proper system boot
    • Prevent data corruption
    • Improve system reliability

    Slide 26 PPT 3: SoC Infrastructure & Reset
    Interrupts
    • There are many situations where other tasks can be performed while waiting for an I/O device to become ready.

      • The event that causes the interruption is called interrupt and the special routine executed to service the interrupt is called ISR.

    • A hardware signal called an Interrupt will alert the processor when an I/O device becomes ready.
    • Interrupt-signal is sent on the interrupt-request line.
    • 25
    Slide 27 PPT 3: SoC Infrastructure & Reset
    25
    • The processor can be performing its own task without the need to continuously check the I/O-device.
    • The routine executed in response to an interrupt-request is called
    • ISR.
    • The processor must inform the device that its request has been recognized by sending INTA signal
    • .
    • For example, consider COMPUTE
    • and
    • PRINT routines (Figure 3.6).
    Slide 28 PPT 3: SoC Infrastructure & Reset
    25
    • The processor first completes the execution of instruction
    • i
    • .
    • Then, the processor loads the PC with the address of the first instruction of the ISR.
    • After the execution of ISR, the processor has to come back to instruction i+1.
    • Therefore, when an interrupt occurs, the current content of the PC is put in the temporary storage location.
    • A return at the end of ISR reloads the PC from that temporary storage location.
    • This causes the execution to resume at instruction i+1.
    • When the processor is handling interrupts, it must inform the device that its request has been recognized.
    • This may be accomplished by INTA signal
    • .
    Slide 29 PPT 3: SoC Infrastructure & Reset
    25
    • S
    • ubroutine
    • ISR
    • A subroutine performs a function required by the program from
    • where
    • it is called
    • ISR may not have anything in common with program being executed at time INTR is received.
    • Subroutine is just a linkage of 2 or more function
    • related to each other.
    • Interrupt is a mechanism for coordinating I/O transfers.
    • The task of saving and restoring the information can be done automatically by the processor.
    • The processor saves only the contents of PC & Status register.
    • Saving registers also increases the Interrupt Latency.
    • Interrupt Latency
    • is a delay between time an interrupt request is received and start the execution of the
    • ISR
    • .•
    • Generally, the long interrupt latency is unacceptable.

    It is possible that sometimes,the processor does not care to be interrupted by certain peripherals.
    • The interrupts from such peripherals may be masked selectively by using registers provided for the purpose.

    • Maskable
    • Interrupt(NMI) which is used for tasks of great importance.

    Slide 33 PPT 3: SoC Infrastructure & Reset
    Many embedded systems are not active all the time.
    • Mobile phones are inactive until a call comes or we want it to do something.
    • So the best way to use such a device is to let it go to sleep and wake it up only when we need some service from it.
    • That can be done very efficiently using the concept of interrupts.

    • I

    • .
    • It is used to control power delivered to a load by adjusting the duty cycle of the
    • signal
    • i

      • e. Instead of reducing voltage directly (which wastes power), PWM switches ON and OFF rapidly to efficiently control power.

    Slide 17 PPT 1: Peripherals, Memory & Endianness
    THE READ Cycle of DRAM:
    • The
    • memory controller associated with DRAM has the function of splitting the address into two, as rows and
    • columns. The

      • address supplied by the processor is split into two and is given to the DRAM address lines in time multiplexed form, as row and column address. The DRAM controller generates the control signals for reading and writing to the DRAM.

    • 25
    • There are two types of flash, namely NOR and NAND
    • .
    • In
    • NOR flash, words can be read from any random location (see Fig.
    • 2.25(a)).
    • It is called NOR flash because it exhibits NOR logic.
    • I .e
    • if one of the word lines is taken high, the corresponding gate transistor pulls the output bit line to a low.

      • In NAND flash, the transistors are connected in series. Only if all word lines are pulled high, the output bit line is pulled low. This is essentially NAND logic, and hence the name (see Fig.

    • 2.25(b)).

    • The application
    • are as
    • of NAND and NOR logic are different
    • .
    • NOR

      • flash can be read randomly, it is used for storing BIOS in PCs, operating systems in mobile phones, tablets, and boot code in general embedded systems, etc

    • .

      • NAND flash is used where serial reading and retrieval is sufficient and is used for storage in USB sticks, cameras, mobile phones, etc.

    • The steps are summarized as follows
    • 1.Send the start signal. This is done by master.
    • 2.Send the slave address.
    • 3.Send the Read/Write signal.
    • 4.Wait for send an acknowledge bit(ACK).
    • 5.Send/Receive the data byte(8 bits)(DATA).
    • 6.Expect/Send acknowledge bit(ACK).
    • 7.Send the Stop Bit.

    • I2C
    • requires only two pins to perform serial data transfer.
    • The ACK signal ensures good synchronization between the transmitter and receiver.
    • Earlier version of I2C had only a speed 100KHZ,but now the I2C specifications supports up to 3.4 MHZ clock speed.
    • This protocol can be used in a
    • muti
    • master environment.
    • The positive aspects of this protocol are follows
    • :

    Slide 51 PPT 1: Peripherals, Memory & Endianness
    Data is sent serially on the MOSI and MISO pins with the LSB first.
    • Fig 2.32 shows the MISO and MOSI signals active for sending data in both the directions.

      • Each SPI transfer is full duplex meaning the data is sent from the master to the slave and from slave to the master at the same time.

    • Because the SPI transactions occur through a shift register arrangement shown in fig
    • 2.32
    • If the master is sending data to a slave, the slave cannot stop sending data back to the master at the same time.

      • This happens because in protocols like SPI, data transfer happens simultaneously in both directions whenever the clock runs

    • It will then send dummy bytes with all 0’s or all 1’s as data.
    Slide 52 PPT 1: Peripherals, Memory & Endianness
    Transmission
    • is in one direction only.
    • If the master is reading data in for a slave, the slave will know to ignore the data being sent by the master
    • .
    • Even if the slave does not have useful data to send, it must still transmit something.
    • So the slave sends dummy data such as:
    • A
    • ll
    • 0’s (00000000) or
    • A
    • ll
    • 1’s (11111111).

    • The figure shows data being sent on both the MOSI and MISO pins.

    Slide 60 PPT 1: Peripherals, Memory & Endianness
    3.6
    • FLAGS
    • All processors have status flags to indicate something about the result of an operation.
    • Each flag is a flip flop which is either set or reset.
    • The one or zero state of flags may be needed before a conditional execution.
    • The flag bits are generally seen in some status register of the processor.
    • In 8051, flags bits are available in a register called the Processor Status Word.
    • In ARM, there is a register called CPSR (Current Program Status Register
    • ),
    • which holds the flag bits
    • .

    • a
    • type

    • is
    • fast
    • in
    • response
    • and
    • low
    • in

    • It
    • is
    • used
    • to
    • store
    • a
    • copy
    • of
    • important
    • data
    • and
    • code
    • which
    • needs
    • to
    • be
    • accessed
    • at
    • a
    • rapid
    • rate.
    • When the required information is found in the cache is called a hit. Otherwise, it is a cache miss.
    • It is not possible to guarantee that a required item will be found in cache when it is needed.
    • Caches are not preferred for time critical applications where delays cannot be tolerated.
    • In such systems ,a Tightly Coupled Memory(TCM) is considered to be a better option.
    • 23
    • 06

    Slide 12 PPT 1: ARM7 Registers & Core
    Cache
    • 23
    • 06
    • 20
    • 2
    • 0
    • PRASHANTH
    • KAMBLI,
    • DEPT.
    • OF
    • ISE,
    • RIT
    • 27
    • In TCM’s critical information(which is pre decided) is stored and used without the indeterminacy of caches.
    • Cache storage is dynamic.
    • It stores information that is likely to be frequently needed.
    • Thus, the content of cache is continuously changed according to the decisions of what is considered important.
    • TCMs contain static information that is predetermined to be very important .
    • Critical procedures, interrupt routines
    • etc
    • are usually TCM hold.

    • E
    • ntered

    • E

    • ntered

    • E
    • ntered

    • P
    • rivileged
    • mode of operation.

    • as

    Slide 57 PPT 1: ARM7 Registers & Core
    Undefined
    • instructions and software interrupts are mutually exclusive.
    • If an instruction is decoded and found to be an
    • SWI(
    • software
    • interrupt
    • instruction
    • ),
    • it cannot be an undefined instruction.
    • A data abort exception occurs at the same time as an FIQ.
    • The processor first enters the data abort handler and then immediately after that proceeds to the FIQ vector.
    • A normal return from FIQ will cause the data abort handler to resume execution.

      • It is necessary to place the data abort exception at a higher priority than FIQ to ensure that the data transfer error does not escape detection.

    • All Exceptions cannot occur at the same time

    ARM9 and ARM10

    ARM9 processor is a 5 stage (five clock cycle) ARM state pipeline is used, consisting of FETCH, DECODE, EXECUTE, MEMORY and WRITE BACK stages.

  • ARM10 processor is a 6 stage consisting of FETCH ,ISSUE, DECODE,EXECUTE, MEMORY ,WRITE stages.

    4.5.3.1 USING THE TLB
    • If the TLB does not contain the physical address corresponding

      • to the generated virtual address, the ‘Address Translation Logic’ which is referred to as ‘translation table walk hardware’ takes over.

    • This action calculates the physical address corresponding to the given logical(virtual)address.
    • Memory calculation is done after the physical address is calculated.
    • The
    • results of address calculation is also placed in the TLB, overwriting an existing value there in.

    • ASSEMBLY
    • PROGRAMMING
    • The processor can opt to use the big endian or little endian format
    • In our discussion here, we choose the little endian format.

    • Data alignment is necessary for efficiency.

    Slide 24 PPT 2: ARM7 Instruction Set
    EXAMPLE
    • 1
    • What is the content of the destination registers after the execution of the following instructions
    • ?
    • R1 = 0
    • R2 =
    • 0x896700FF
    • MOV R3, R1
    • MVN R4, R1
    • MVN R5, R2
    • MOV R6, #235
    • SOLUTION
    • R3 = 0
    • R4 = 0xFFFFFFFF
    • R5 = 0x7698FF00
    • R6 = 0x000000EB
    • (235 in 32-bit hexadecimal format)

    • It’s used for:

    • ASSEMBLY
    • PROGRAMMING
    • The processor can opt to use the big endian or little endian format
    • In our discussion here, we choose the little endian format.
    • 8-bit (byte), 16-bit(half word) and 32-bit(word) data sizes are possible.
    • Data alignment is necessary for efficiency.

    Slide 24 PPT 1: LPC2148 Peripherals (Timers & UART)
    EXAMPLE
    • 1
    • What is the content of the destination registers after the execution of the following instructions
    • ?
    • R1 = 0
    • R2 =
    • 0x896700FF
    • MOV R3, R1
    • MVN R4, R1
    • MVN R5, R2
    • MOV R6, #235
    • SOLUTION
    • R3 = 0
    • R4 = 0xFFFFFFFF
    • R5 = 0x7698FF00
    • R6 = 0x000000EB
    • (235 in 32-bit hexadecimal format)

    5.3 Assembly language programming
    • Now we can start assembly programming using the
    • Keil
    • Microvision
    • V assembler.
    • The step-by-step method of using this IDE for Assembly Language
    Slide 53 PPT 1: LPC2148 Peripherals (Timers & UART)
    5.3.1 Assembler
    • Directives
    • For each assembler, there are 'directives'.

      • They are specific for an assembler and are non-executable statements, which means that they don't cause any `execution' by the processor.

    • Directives are related solely to the assembler.
    • We need a few directives of the
    • Keil
    • Assembler to get started in assembly programming.
    Slide 54 PPT 1: LPC2148 Peripherals (Timers & UART)
    5.3.1.1 AREA
    • The
    • “AREA”
    • directive instructs the assembler to assemble a new code or data section".
    • The above is a statement given in the user manual of
    • Keil
    • Microvi­sion
    • .
    • It implies that the assembler understands two types of information —code and data.
    • Data is usually stored in R/W memory (RAM) while code is in Read only memory (ROM).
    • The assembler requires the names of these areas of code or data.
    Slide 55 PPT 1: LPC2148 Peripherals (Timers & UART)
    Typical statements that use the AREA directive are
    • AREA LINEAR, CODE, Read only
    • AREA NOTE, DATA, R/W

      • It is not mandatory to use the words 'Read Only' and `R/W' because by default, data is in R/W memory and code is in Read Only Memory.

    • Code is always in ROM, though data is also allowed to be stored there.

      • As a matter of fact, the R/W memory is volatile and is used only for intermediate storage of data in the course of instruction execution

    Slide 56 PPT 1: LPC2148 Peripherals (Timers & UART)
    5.3.1.2 ENTRY
    • The ENTRY directive marks the first instruction to be executed within an application.

      • Because an application cannot have more than one entry point, the ENTRY directive can appear in only one of the source modules.

    Slide 57 PPT 1: LPC2148 Peripherals (Timers & UART)
    5.3.1.3 END
    • This directive is the last line in an assembly module and it indicates that the assembler need not read
    • beyondthat
    • line.
    Slide 58 PPT 1: LPC2148 Peripherals (Timers & UART)
    5.3.1.4 DCB, DCW
    • and
    • DCD
    • The ARM processor has a word length of 32 bits, but it can handle data of 8 bits (byte) and 16 bits (half word) also.
    • The
    • Keil
    • assembler, however, uses the notations of byte, word and double word for 8 bits, 16 bits and 32 bits, respectively.
    • The directives used are DCB for data byte, DCW for data word and DCD for data double word.
    • The following lines show how these directives are used.
    • These directives are used by the assembler to store data in ROM.
    Slide 59 PPT 1: LPC2148 Peripherals (Timers & UART)
    Example
    • B
    • AG DCB 0x32, 56, Ox7E
    • BAG DCW Ox2234, Ox ED37, Ox9067
    • BOG DCD Ox DE345678,
    • 0
    • x34009812
    • The first line says that three bytes are to be stored in contiguous loca­
    • tions with labels, RAG, RAG+1, and RAG+2. Recollect that each address
    • always corresponds to one byte of data. The second line indicates that 16
    • bit data is being stored in locations BAG, BAG+2 and BAG+4. The third
    • line states that 32 bit data is to be stored in addresses with labels BOG
    • and BOG+4.
    Slide 60 PPT 1: LPC2148 Peripherals (Timers & UART)
    5.3.1.5
    • equ
    • The EQU directive is for equating a label to a constant.
    • When the label is encountered by the assembler during the process of assembly, it is replaced by its actual value.
    • Examples of the usage of this directive are
    • RAPID EQU 65
    • STRT_ADDR EQU Ox84000000
    Slide 61 PPT 1: LPC2148 Peripherals (Timers & UART)
    AREA ODD, CODE, READONLY
    • ENTRY
    • MOV R1,#1
    • ;R1=1
    • MOV R2,#9
    • ;R2=9, the counter
    • MOV R3,#1
    • ;R3=1
    • BACKK ADD R3,R3,#2 ;R3 has the odd numbers
    • ADD R1,R1,R3 ;R1 contains the final sum
    • SUBS R2,R2,#1 ;R2 is the counter for 10 numbers
    • BNE BACKK ;repeat the addition until R2=0
    • GO B GO
    • END
    Slide 62 PPT 1: LPC2148 Peripherals (Timers & UART)
    5.4
    • Accessing
    • memory
    • We already know that ARM is a load-store architecture.
    • This means that only the load and store instructions access memory directly.
    • There are load/store instructions with single registers, as well as with multiple registers.
    Slide 63 PPT 1: LPC2148 Peripherals (Timers & UART)
    5.4.1 Single register data transfer

    The word 'load' indicates that data from memory is to be moved to a register. `Store' means that data from a register is saved in memory

  • The format for a load/store instruction is
  • LDR/STR {<
  • cond
  • >},

    • The operation of load/ store has a register Rd in which the data is placed. The `addressing mode' implies that an effective address is generated by address calculation.

    Slide 64 PPT 1: LPC2148 Peripherals (Timers & UART)
    Example
    • LDR R2, [R7]
    • ; copy into R2, the content of memory, as pointed by R7
    • STR R2, [R7]
    • ; store the content of R2, into the address pointed by R7
    • The address of the memory location for the load/store operation is placed in a
    • register, called the base register. The address may be generated and put into
    • the base register in various calculation formats using shifting, adding offsets,
    • summing the contents of more registers etc.
    Slide 65 PPT 1: LPC2148 Peripherals (Timers & UART)
    Find the effective address of memory as specified by the following instructions
    • LDR R4, [R7, ROR #4]
    • STR R7, [R3, R4, LSL #2]
    • LDR R6, [R2, #4]
    • STR R8, [R1, R2, ASR #6]
    • LDR R1,[R7,R6]
    Slide 66 PPT 1: LPC2148 Peripherals (Timers & UART)
    The effective address is R7=R7 rotated right 4 times.
    • The effective address is R3=R3+R4 rotated left logically 2 times.
    • The effective address is R2=R2+4
    • The effective address is R1+R2 right shifted (arithmetically) 6 times
    • The effective address is R7=R7+R6
    Slide 67 PPT 1: LPC2148 Peripherals (Timers & UART)
    5.4.2 Loading/Storing data of different lengths
    • Data to be loaded or stored may be in bytes, half words (16 bits) or words (32 bits).
    • Data may be signed or unsigned, as well.

      • To make this explicit, the load and store instructions may be appended with the characters, B for byte, H for half word and S for signed.

    Slide 68 PPT 1: LPC2148 Peripherals (Timers & UART)
    Instruction
    • Meaning
    • LDR
    • Load Word
    • LDRH
    • Load Half Word
    • LDRSH
    • Load Signed Half Word
    • LDRB
    • Load Byte
    • LDRSB
    • Load Signed Byte
    • STR
    • Store Word
    • STRH
    • Store Half Word
    • STRB
    • Store Byte
    • Load/Store instructions
    Slide 69 PPT 1: LPC2148 Peripherals (Timers & UART)
    How is data accessed from memory with the following instructions, given that R7=0x00004400 and that memory contents are as given in Table
    • (assume the little endian scheme)?
    • Address
    • Data
    • 0x00004400
    • 89
    • 0x00004401
    • E6
    • 0x00004402
    • F3
    • 0x00004403
    • 94
    • LDR R3, [R7]
    • LDRB R3,[R7]
    • LDRH R3, [R7]
    • LDRSH R3, [R7]
    Slide 70 PPT 1: LPC2148 Peripherals (Timers & UART)
    LDR R3,
    • [R7]

      • This instruction pertains to a word (32 bits) of data to be loaded into the destination register. After the execution of the above instruction, R3=0x94F3E689.

    • LDRB
    • R3, [R7]

      • After the execution of the above instruction, R3=0x00000089. This is because only a byte of the data will be taken from memory and so the lowest byte of the word (in the lowest address) pointed by R7, is loaded to R3.

    • LDRH R3, [R7]

      • After the execution of the above instruction, R3=0x0000E689. This is because the half word of the data will be taken from memory and the lower 16 bits of the word pointed by R7, is loaded to R3.

    • LDRSH R3, [R7]

      • After the execution of the above instruction, R3=OxFFFFE689. The lower half word of the data is OxE689. The instruction LDRSH considers this to be a 'signed

    • halfword
    • ' and when loading it to a 32-bit register, the sign bit is extended to the upper 16 bits.
    Slide 71 PPT 1: LPC2148 Peripherals (Timers & UART)
    The content of registers, R6 and R3, and memory are as given below. R6= 0x40002200, R3=0x3498CE56

    Slide 73 PPT 1: LPC2148 Peripherals (Timers & UART)
    5.4.3 Pre Indexing, Post Indexing and Write Back
    • Consider the Instruction
    • STR R0, [R7,#2] and LDR R0,[R7,ROR #4]
    • In the first instruction, it calculates the effective address as R7=R7+2.
    • In the second instruction, it calculates the address as R7=R7 ROR #4.
    Slide 74 PPT 1: LPC2148 Peripherals (Timers & UART)
    What is the content of R7 after the execution of previous instruction?
    • The content of R7 remains the same as it was before the instruction execution.
    • Means the address calculation before, no change in R7
    Slide 75 PPT 1: LPC2148 Peripherals (Timers & UART)
    Write Back(!):
    • If it necessary to save the effective address into the base registers,
    • Instruction should have the concept of “ Write Back”.
    • Means the instruction need to have with the sign “ !”.
    • Write back instruction is:
    • STR R0, [R7,#2]! and LDR R0,[R7,ROR #4]!
    Slide 76 PPT 1: LPC2148 Peripherals (Timers & UART)
    Examples

    What is the content of the base register R6 after the execution of each 0f the following instructions, given that R6=0x44001100 and R2 = 0x02.

  • LDR R2,[R6,#0x0056]!
  • STR
  • R1,[
  • R6,R2]!
  • LDR R3,[R6,LSL #4)
    • Slide 77 PPT 1: LPC2148 Peripherals (Timers & UART)
    1 . LDR R2,[R6,#0x0056]!

    The effective address is calculated as R6= R6+0x0056 = Ox44001 156, because of write back, this number will be saved in R6 after instruction execution.

  • 2.
  • STR
  • R1,[
  • R6,R2]!

    • The effective address is calculated as R6=R6+R2 = Ox44001102 because of write back, this number will be saved in R6 after instruction execution.

  • 3
  • . LDR R3,[R6,LSL #4]

    • The effective address is calculated as R6=R6, LSL# 4. Because the instruction does not have the write back feature (!), after instruction execution, R6 will retain its previous content of Ox44001100.

    Slide 78 PPT 1: LPC2148 Peripherals (Timers & UART)
    5.4.4 Post Indexing
    • In this mode, the effective address calculation is done after the execution of the specific instruction.
    • Thus, write back is inherently implied.
    • Take the case of the instruction LDR R2, [R7], #2
    • Here the data pointed by the content of R7 is first copied to R2.
    • After that, the content of R7 (which is the base register) is changed to R7 +2.
    Slide 79 PPT 1: LPC2148 Peripherals (Timers & UART)
    5.4.5 Immediate mode of addressing
    • This is the mode when an immediate data is used as an operand.
    • Consider the instruction
    • MOV R1, #10
    • ; move data 10 to R1
    • ADD R4, R2, #3
    • ; Add 3 to the content of R2, and save the sum in R4
    • These instructions seems to be ok. But there is an unseen issues involved
    Slide 80 PPT 1: LPC2148 Peripherals (Timers & UART)
    What is the biggest constant that can be used in the immediate mode?
    • All instructions of ARM are to have same size 32 bits
    • If an immediate data is to be included, it must fit in the 32-Bit size allocated for a data processing instruction.
    • Means the larger sizes of constants cannot be used in immediate data.
    Slide 81 PPT 1: LPC2148 Peripherals (Timers & UART)
    The figure 5.10 shows the format of a typical data processing instructions of ARM.
    • If bit 25 is’0’ ,it is understood that the operand2 is a register.
    • If bit 25 is’1’,the operand2 is immediate data.
    • 12bit space is allocated for operand2.
    Slide 82 PPT 1: LPC2148 Peripherals (Timers & UART)
    The 12bit space is arranged as a four bit rotate value and an eight bit immediate value as shown in figure 5.11.
    • The rotation bit field is only 4 bits .

      • Internally this rotation field is multiplied by 2,and thus becomes the case of an 8 bit number multiplied an even number of times to get a new number.

    • The operation must be done in a 32 bit space.
    Slide 83 PPT 1: LPC2148 Peripherals (Timers & UART)
    When we want larger constant, it is not possible to write it in an instruction like MOV R1, #n, where n is constant
    • Need to use the immediate mode in such a way that the constant that we need is obtained by the ARM rotation scheme
    • Not possible by programmer to find out exact 8-bit number which will produce a bigger size constant
    • So Assembler will come to an assistant here.
    • for that, a pseudo instruction is provided
    • The format is LDR Rd, =constant ; where Rd is destination register
    • ex: LDR R1, =0x44444444
    Slide 84 PPT 1: LPC2148 Peripherals (Timers & UART)
    But all constants are not obtainable by such a scheme.
    • Consider 0x77777777.
    • This constant cannot be generated in this way.

      • In such a case, the assembler is used to store the required constant in memory and take it from memory using the LDR instruction.

    • The area in memory where such constants are loaded is called a “literal pool”.
    • programmer need only write the pseudo instruction.
    • The assembler
    • wil
    • handle the matter in an efficient manner.
    • The sequence of actions that follow is highlighted below.
    • Take the case of needing a constant 0x77777777.
    • The pseudo instruction we write is
    • LDR R1
    • ,=0x77777777
    Slide 85 PPT 1: LPC2148 Peripherals (Timers & UART)
    Space for literal pool
    • is:
    • After the END directive, so that it can be stored in ROM in the space after the code.

      • If the program is large and the pseudo instruction for it is far away from the end of the program, the literal pool may be beyond the range of +/-4KB.

    • In such cases a directive LTORG(Origin of literal pool) is used.
    • This directive may be included in the program to specify where we want the literal pool to be.
    • Placing LTORG in this place gives a warning message ‘Literal pool in executable area’.
    Slide 86 PPT 1: LPC2148 Peripherals (Timers & UART)
    Literal pool are to be place in location in such a way that the processor does not consider them as instructions.
    • They are best placed after unconditional branch instructions.
    Slide 87 PPT 1: LPC2148 Peripherals (Timers & UART)
    5.4.6 Read Only and Read/Write Memory
    • We know that ROM is for code and RAM is for temporary storage of data.
    • It is important to realize that data in RAM is accessed using instructions of the processor
    • while data is placed in ROM using assembler directives like DCD, DCB etc.
    • From ROM data can be brought to RAM by
    • fisrt
    • loading it to registers using Load (LDR) instructions
    • Later store them using STR instructions
    Slide 88 PPT 1: LPC2148 Peripherals (Timers & UART)
    Block data transfer using multiple registers:

    This ISA allows multiple registers to be loaded from contiguous memory locations, with the use of just one instructions.

    Similarly ,the content of Multiple registers may be saved into consecutive locations in memory. Such instructions are referred to as multiple load/store instructions.

  • LDM {
  • cond
  • }address-mode Rn{!},
  • reg
  • list{^}
  • Cond: is an optional condition
  • address-mode: specifies how the memory is sequenced.
  • Rn: is the base register for the load operation.
  • ! : Specifies base register write back
  • i.e

    • the address in the base register is updated after the transfer.(if the base register is in the register list then no need to specify write back)

  • Reg
  • list: List of symbolic register names and register ranges enclosed in braces.There must be at least one register in the list.
  • ^ :This is not to be used in the user/system mode. It is meant for use in the privileged mode
  • .
    • Slide 89 PPT 1: LPC2148 Peripherals (Timers & UART)
    Addressing modes of the multiple load/store instructions:
    • These modes indicates how to change the memory address after each word transfer.
    • The addressing modes are:
    • IA:Increment
    • After
    • IB :Increment before
    • DA:Decrement
    • After
    • DB:Decrement
    • Before
    • Instructions are
    • LDMIA R7! {R0-R5}
    Slide 90 PPT 1: LPC2148 Peripherals (Timers & UART)
    The STM instruction:
    • Syntax: STM{
    • cond
    • } address-mode Rn!, reg-list{^}
    • The operation is just the reverse of the LDM instruction.
    • Here the contents of the registers are stored in memory location indexed by the base register.
    • STMDB
    • R1!,{R7,R2-R4
    • } if R1=
    • 0X44400088
    • The
    • base register is R1,It contains the number 0X44400088.Since the DB suffix is used
    • , The
    • R1 register is decremented by 4 and it contains the number 0X44400084.
    • The data in R7 is saved to this location.
    • The content of R1 is decremented by 4 and the data in R4 is save in this address.
    • These operation of decrementing the base register is repeated for storing the data in R3 and R2.
    • At the end of the execution of the instruction, the value in R1 is the final updated
    • value because write back is stipulated.
    Slide 91 PPT 1: LPC2148 Peripherals (Timers & UART)

    Slide 3 PPT 2: Embedded C and Interfacing
    Features of LPC 214x series
    • 16-bit/32-bit ARM7TDMI-S microcontroller in a tiny LQFP64 package
    • 8 kB to 40 kB of on-chip static RAM and 32kB-512 kB of on-chip flash memory
    • 128-bit wide interface/accelerator enables high-speed 60 MHz operation

      • In-system programming/Application Programming via on-chip boot loader software. Single flash sector or full chip erase in

    • ms.
    • The LPC2146/2148 provide 8kb of on chip RAM accessible to USB by DMA.

      • Embedded ICE RT and Embedded Trace interfaces offer real-time debugging with the on-chip Real Monitor software and high-speed tracing of instructions execution.

    • USB 2.0 Full-speed compliant device controller with 2kB of endpoint RAM

      • One or two 10-bit ADCs provide a total of 6/14 analog inputs with conversion times as slow 2.44micro seconds per channel.

    • Single 10-bit DAC providers provides variable analog output.
    • Two 32-bit timers/external event counters, PWM unit and watchdog.
    • Low power Real-Time Clock (RTC) with independent power and 32 kHz clock input.

      • Multiple serial interfaces including two UARTs, two Fast 12C bus, SPI and SSP with buffering and variable data length capabilities

    • Vectored Interrupt Controller (VIC) with configurable priorities and vector addresses.
    • Up to 45 of 5 V tolerant fast general purpose I/O pins in a tiny LQFP64 package
    • Up to 21 external interrupt pins available

    Slide 13 PPT 2: Embedded C and Interfacing
    Crystal oscillator and PLL
    • There are various options for the oscillator which supplies the system clock.
    • External frequency by connecting an external oscillator, supplying a 50% duty cycle signal in the range of 1-50 MHz

      • There is also an on-board oscillator and for that an external crystal with frequency in the range of 1-30 MHz is allowed.

    • There are two PLLs and on is used entirely for the generation of the system clock, noted as CCLK.

      • The PLL accepts an input clock frequency in the range of 10-25MHz, which is multiplied to get a range of 10-60 MHz for the core clock

    • The core clock is used entirely for generation of system clock noted as CCLK.

      • The PLL accepts an input clock frequency in the range of 10MHz to 25MHz only, which is multiplied to get a range of 10-60MHz for the core clock.

    • 13
    Slide 14 PPT 2: Embedded C and Interfacing
    VPB divider
    • Volume Parameter Block(VPB)
    • Usually, peripherals do not work at the high frequency that the core operates at.
    • So the core clock is generally slowed down to one half or one fourth for use by peripherals.
    • The default condition at reset is for the VPB bus to run at one quarter speed.
    • The VPB divider determines the relationship between the processor core clock CCLK and the peripheral clock PCLK.
    • Another reason to do the frequency division is to reduce power dissipation.
    • 14
    Slide 15 PPT 2: Embedded C and Interfacing
    VPB divider connection
    • 15
    Slide 16 PPT 2: Embedded C and Interfacing
    Low power modes
    • The LPC214x supports two low power modes
    • Idle mode
    • Power-down mode.
    • In idle mode, instruction execution is suspended until either a Reset or interrupt occurs.

      • Peripherals can continue to operate are during Idle mode and may generate interrupts to cause the processor to resume execution.

    • This mode eliminates power used by the processor itself, memory systems and related controllers and internal buses.
    • In power-down mode, the oscillator is shut down and the chip receives no internal clocks.

      • The processor state and registers, peripheral registers and internal SRAM values are preserved throughout this mode and the logic levels of chip pins remain unchanged.

    • This state can be exited and normal operation can be resumed by Reset or interrupt.
    • Chip power consumption is nearly zero.
    • 16
    Slide 17 PPT 2: Embedded C and Interfacing
    Wake up timer

    If the processor has gone to any of the low power modes, it will be awakened by a stimuli like an interrupt or reset signal.

  • When wake up occurs, code execution for the required application should start.
  • The function of the wakeup timer is to monitor the crystal oscillator to ensure that it is safe to begin code execution
  • Once a clock is detected wakeup timer counts 4096 clocks.
  • When the onboard modules initialization is complete, the processor is allowed to execute instructions.
  • The function of the wakeup timer is to produce a standard amount of delay.
  • 17
    • Slide 18 PPT 2: Embedded C and Interfacing
    RESET
    • There are two sources for Reset.
    • One is the reset pin and other is the watchdog reset
    • The RESET pin is a Schmitt Trigger input
    • Reset occurs by one of these sources, the first action is of the starting of the wakeup timer

      • The process of resetting continues until the on-chip circuitry is initialized and a fixed number of clocks have occurred after the oscillator starts running.

    Internal Bus
    • Fig 6.6 shows the internal bus structure of the LPC214X family.
    • The fastest bus which is the ARM local bus and it connects the ARM core to memory.
    • Any high-speed GPIO may also use this bus.

      • The system functions and the VIC (Vectored Interrupt Controller) are on AHB bus, while the peripherals use the low-speed VPB bus.

    • Bridges are interfacing circuitry which convert one bus standard to another.
    • Thus there is an AHB bridge which acts as an intermediary between the highest speed local bus and the AHB.
    • The other bridge is the AHB to VPB bridge.

    Using GPIO pins
    • 30
    • Refer figure 6.7 from text book, which shows the conceptual view of chip with port1 I/O pins.

      • As mentioned earlier that, pins 0-15 of port 1 are not available, pins p1.16 to p1.25 are reserved. Compared to port 1, for port 0, more number of pins are available for use as I/O

    • Each pin can be used for four different functions ,but the default functionality is as a GPIO pin.
    • It can be addressed individually and has the following attributes associated with it:
    • It may be an input or output pin
    • If it is an input pin, it reads in value (0 or 1) from outside the chip.
    • If it is an output pin, it may be set or cleared by software.
    Slide 31 PPT 2: Embedded C and Interfacing
    Using GPIO pins
    • There are four registers associates with each GPIO
    • A direction register (IODIR) in which we can decide whether it is to be an output or input pin

      • If the pin is an output pin, there is a ‘Set’ register (IOSET) in which we can make a pin to have a logic level of ‘1’ on it.

      If the pin is an output pin, a ‘clear’ register (IOCLR) is available in which we can make a pin to have a logic level of ‘0’ on it.

    • A ‘PIN’ register (IOPIN) register in which we can read the actual logic levels on the pin.
    • We can use this register for doing set/clear functions for the output port, but not on individual pins.

    Slide 34 PPT 2: Embedded C and Interfacing
    IPOIN register
    • Port pins that are configured to perform only digital functions.

      • It will give the logic value of the pin irrespective of the pin whether the pin is an input or output, and as GPIO or an alternate digital function.

      If a pin has an analog function as one of its options , the pin state cannot be read if the analog configuration is selected.

      Selecting the pin as an analog input disconnects the digital features of the pin .In that case , the pin value read in the IOPIN register is not valid.

      It is possible to write to the IOPIN register and then it stores the value in the port output register, bypassing the need to use both IOSET and IOCLR registers to obtain entire written value.

    • This feature should be used carefully in an application since it affects the entire port.

      • The IOPIN register may be used to write a value in the IO port register. But it cannot be used for toggling individual pin outputs.

    Slide 40 PPT 2: Embedded C and Interfacing
    6.1.5.2 Timers
    • The LPC214x family has two Timer/Counter units – Timer 0 and Timer 1, both of which are identical in features.
    • It is designed to count cycles of the peripheral clock (PCLK) or an externally-supplied clock.
    • In the PCLK unit is timer and in external clock unit is counter.

      • The timer module can optionally generate interrupts or perform other actions at specified time values, based on four match registers.

      It also includes four capture inputs to trap the timer value when an input signal transitions, optionally generating an interrupt.

    • 40
    Slide 41 PPT 2: Embedded C and Interfacing
    Operation as an interval timer
    • Each timer is 32-bit Timer/Counter with a programmable 32-bit
    • Prescaler
    • .
    • The steps are as follows:
    • A number is loaded into a ‘Timer Count’ register (TC)
    • There is a match register into which another number is loaded
    • The Timer Count Register is given a Count Enable (CE) signal and then it starts counting up, once per cycle of PCLK

      • The TC register value increases, and when it becomes equal to the number in the match register, we say that a ‘match’ occurs.

    • The event that follows the ‘match’ can be predecided by bit settings in the match control register(MCR).
    • The possibilities are
    • Stop Timer: Generate interrupt if operating in the interrupt mode
    • Reset timer count register Generate interrupt if operating in the interrupt mode
    • Continuous operation with optional interrupt generation on match
    • 41
    Slide 42 PPT 2: Embedded C and Interfacing
    Registers for using the timer in the polled mode
    • 42
    Slide 43 PPT 2: Embedded C and Interfacing
    Operation as an interval timer
    • 43
    Slide 44 PPT 2: Embedded C and Interfacing
    6.1.5.2.2
    • Registers
    • for timing operation

      • There are two timers Timer0 and Timer1.For each timer , there are separate set of registers which are identified by adding 0 or 1 to the register name.

    • TIMER COUNT (TC) REGISTER: This register increments by 1 for every PCLK or every pre-scaled PCLK.
    • TIMER CONTROL REGISTER (TCR): This register controls the above mentioned Timer Count Register.
    • There are two such registers, corresponding to Timer0 and Timer1
    • i.e
    • T0CR and T1CR.
    • TC can be enabled, disabled and reset using the TCR.
    • Setting bit0, enables counting
    • i.e
    • count enable signal to start counting
    • Bit1 is reset bit and when it is made 1 the Timer Count Register is reset at the next cycle of PCLK
    • 44
    Slide 45 PPT 2: Embedded C and Interfacing
    Registers for timing operation

    MATCH REGISTERS – MR0 to MR3: There are four match registers, which are 32 bits in length and can be loaded with numbers.

  • One of them can be used for a timer operation.

    • When the timer count register increments to become equal to the number in the match register, a match is said to have occurred.

    MATCH CONTROL REGISTERS (MCR): The bit configuration of the Match Control Register determines the course of action following a ‘match’.

    Slide 49 PPT 2: Embedded C and Interfacing
    Frequency Calculation
    • For calculation of the time period, we need a reference clock signal CCLK.
    • Let use CCLK of 50 MHz it is divided by 4, to get a PCLK of 12.5
    • MHz.
    • This translates to a PCLK period of T 0.08 µs.
    • The wait function creates a delay. The amount of delay is dependent on the number loaded in the Match register
    • Load a value of 0x34FF (13567) in T0MR1
    • The timer count register starts from 0 and counts up to 13,567 when match occurs.
    • Thus, 13567+1 = 13568 cycles of PCLK have to pass before match occurs.
    • Thus, the delay obtained by this value in T0MR1 is 13567 x 0.08 = 1085 µs = 1.085
    • ms.

    Slide 50 PPT 2: Embedded C and Interfacing
    Frequency Calculation
    • contd

    • During this time, all the pins of Port 0 have one logic level. Then the pin toggles to the alternate logic level.
    • Thus, the time period of the square waveform T, is 1.085 x 2 = 2.170
    • ms.
    • The frequency of the waveform is the reciprocal of the timer = 1/T = 0.460
    • KHz
    • = 460 Hz

    Slide 51 PPT 2: Embedded C and Interfacing
    Vectored Interrupt Controller
    • The Vectored Interrupt Controller (VIC) takes 32 interrupt request inputs and
    • programmably
    • assigns them into three categories – FIQ, Vectored IRQ and non-vectored IRQ.
    • FIQ requests have the highest priority. If more than one request is assigned to FIQ, the VIC does the
    • ORing
    • of the requests to produce FIQ signal to the processor.

      • Vector IRQ have the middle priority, but only 16 of the 32 requests can be assigned to this category. Any of the 32 requests can be assigned to any of the 16 vectored IRQ slots, among which slot 0 has the highest priority and slot 15 has lowest priority.

    • Non-vectored IRQ have the lowest priority
    • The VIC performs
    • ORing
    • of the requests from all the vectored and non vectored IRQ’s to produce the IRQ signal to the ARM processor.
    • 51
    Slide 52 PPT 2: Embedded C and Interfacing
    Interrupt Sources
    • 52

    • 62
    • Functions of PWM:
    • Concept of match registers are used here like timer. There is a match register MR0.
    • The number loaded into MR0 determines the pulse repetition rate of PWM waveform.
    • MR0 determines the time period T of the waveform. There are 6 other match registers MR1 to MR6.
    • They are used for deciding ON period of each of 6 PWM signals PWM1 to PWM6.
    • Fig 6.16 shows a pulse stream in which t is the ON period and
    • T,the
    • pulse
    • period.The
    • duty cycle is defined as
    • Duty cycle = t/T x 100 %

      • If six PWM outputs are used in the single edge controlled scheme, all of them go high at the same time, but the fall time of the pulse are different and determined by the values in registers MR1 to MR6.

    • The functions of each of the bits is shown in fig 6.18.

    Slide 71 PPT 2: Embedded C and Interfacing
    PWM Latch Enable Register (PWMLER)
    • PWM Latch Enable Register (PWMLER)
    • It is an 8-bit register used to control the update of the PWM match registers when they are used for PWM generation.

      • When software writes to the location of a PWM match register while the timer is in PWM mode, the value is held in a shadow register.

      When a PWM Match 0 event occurs, the contents of shadow registers will be transferred to the actual match registers if the corresponding bit in the latch enable register has been set.

      At that point, the new values will take effect and determine the course of the next PWM cycle. Once the transfer of new corresponding bit in the PWMLER is set and a PWM Match 0 event occurs.

    • 71
    Slide 72 PPT 2: Embedded C and Interfacing
    PWM match Registers
    • PWM Match Registers - PWMMR0 – PWMMR6
    • The 32-bit PWM Match register values are continuously compared to the PWM Timer Counter value.
    • When the two values are equal, actions can be triggered automatically.
    • The action possibilities are to generate an interrupt reset the PWM Timer Counter, or stop the timer.

    Universal asynchronous receiver/transmitter
    • A UART is a mandatory unit in every MCU.
    • In LPC 214x chip, there are two UARTs in the chip – UART0 and UART1.
    • The UART is a unit which does serial transmission on the in
    • TxD
    • and serial reception on the pin
    • RxD
    • .
    • For the chip there are two transmit and receive pins each.
    • 74
    Slide 75 PPT 2: Embedded C and Interfacing
    Serial data transmission and reception
    • The mechanism employed in serial data transmission and reception:
    • Inside the MCU, all data communication is done in parallel.
    • Data is moved around as either 32, 16 or 8 bits.

      • Thus, when data is to be transmitted or received serially, there is a parallel to serial (and vice versa) conversion involved.

      For transmission, the data from a buffer register is converted to serial form, framed by adding the start, stop and parity bits and sent on the

    • TxD
    • pin.

      • In reception ,the bits received are kept in a buffer (named the hold register),de-framed, parallelized and moved as one byte to the CPU.

    • Reception and transmission are done at a rate commonly known as the baud rate.
    • There is a baud rate generator inside.
    • Baud rate can be decided by a calculation depending on the value of certain numbers in the UART registers.
    • The UART can work in the polled mode or in the interrupt mode.
    • 75
    Slide 76 PPT 2: Embedded C and Interfacing
    Serial data transmission and reception
    • contd
    • Figure below shows the conceptual view of the UART operation.
    • In transmission, there is a Transmit FIFO in which the bytes to be transmitted are stored.
    • The 8-bit register Transmit Hold Register(THR) is the top byte of the UART transmit FIFO.
    • The top byte is the newest character in the Transmit FIFO and can be written via the bus interface.
    • The LSB represents the first bit to be transmitted.
    • From THR, the data is framed and serialized.
    • The TSR (Transmit Shift Register) does the serialization of data, and sends it out, bit by bit on the
    • TxD
    • pin.
    • 76
    Slide 77 PPT 2: Embedded C and Interfacing
    Serial data transmission and reception
    • contd
    • 77
    Slide 78 PPT 2: Embedded C and Interfacing
    Serial data transmission and reception
    • contd
    • Reception is similar.
    • The received serial data comes in the Receiver Shift Register (RSR)
    • It is
    • deframed
    • , parallelized and saved in the receiver FIFO.
    • The Receiver Buffer Register (RBR) is the top byte of the UART receiver FIFO
    • The top byte of the Receive FIFO contains the oldest character received and can be read via the bus interface.
    • The LSB represents the oldest received bit.
    • If the character received is less than 8 bits, the unused MSBs are padded with zeroes.
    • 78
    Slide 79 PPT 2: Embedded C and Interfacing
    The BAUD Rate Generator
    • Transmission and reception rates are referred to as TCLK and RCLK.
    • These clocks are generated by the BRG (Baud Rate Generator) and these rates can be fixed by a formula.
    • 79
    Slide 80 PPT 2: Embedded C and Interfacing
    Registers of UART0
    • U0DLL and U0DLM – Divisor Latch registers:
    • Both of them hold 8-bit values.
    • These register together from a 16-bit divisor value which is used in baud rate generation.
    • U0DML holds the upper 8-bits and U0DLL holds the lower 8-bits

      • Since these form a divisor value and division by zero is invalid, the starting value for U0DLL is 0x01 (and not 0x00), i.e. the starting value in combined formation is 0x0001.

    • In order to access and use these registers properly, DLAB bit in U0LCR must be first set to 1.
    • The factor MULVAL/(MULVAL + DIVADDVAL) depends on the
    • prescaler
    • and another register.
    • 80
    Slide 81 PPT 2: Embedded C and Interfacing
    Registers of UAER0
    • contd
    • 81
    • The formula for baud rate calculation is
    • Using this formula ,for a system with PCLK=20 MHz,
    • UoDLM
    • =0x00 and
    • UoDLL
    • =0x5D ,DIVADDVAL=2 and MULVAL=5.
    • The UART baud rate=9600bauds.
    Slide 82 PPT 2: Embedded C and Interfacing
    UART0 transmit holding register (U0THR)
    • This is an 8-bit register and part of the transmit buffer.
    • It is the top most byte of this buffer, and new characters are to be loaded into this register for being transmitted.
    • The data to be transmitted is written into this ‘write only’ register.
    • 82
    Slide 83 PPT 2: Embedded C and Interfacing
    UART0 FIFO control registers(U0FCR)
    • This is a 8-bit register
    • Bit 0: E: This bit must be set for enabling the Tx and Rx FIFOs

      • Bit 1: Rx FIFO Reset: This must be set, to clear all bytes in UART0 RX FIFO and reset the pointer logic. This bit is self-clearing.

      Bit 2: Tx FIFO Reset: This must be set to clear all bytes in UART0 Tx FIFO and reset the pointer logic. This bit is self-clearing.

      Bit 7 and 6: Rx trigger level: these two bits determine how many receiver FIFO characters must be written before an interrupt is activated

    • 83
    Slide 84 PPT 2: Embedded C and Interfacing
    UART0 line control register (U0LCR)
    • The U0LCR is an 8-bit register which determines the format of the data character that is to be transmitted or received.
    • The bits used here are:
    • Bit 1: 0: These two bits have been chosen to be ‘11’ to indicate 8-bit character length.
    • Bit 2: This is made ‘0’ to select one stop bit.
    • Bit 7: This is the Divisor Latch Access Bit (DLAB) and is set, to enable the use of divisor latch.
    • Other bits of this register pertain to parity and break control.
    • These have been disabled by making these bits to be ‘0’.
    • Thus we have U0LCR = 1000 0011 = 0x83
    • 84

    Cortex-M Processors Family
    • Cortex- M0:

      • It is very small processor (in size) on account of its very low gate count. It is meant to be used in very low power embedded product designs.

    • The Arm Cortex-M0 processor is one of the smallest Arm processors available.
    Slide 8 PPT 1: ARM Cortex-M0/M0+ & NVIC
    The Cortex-M0 has an exceptionally small silicon area, low power and minimal code footprint, enabling developers to achieve 32-bit performance at an 8-bit price point, bypassing the step to 16-bit devices
    • .
    • The ultra-low gate count of the processor enables its deployment in
    • analog
    • and mixed signal devices.

    Cortex-
    • M0+:

      • It is similar to M0, but can boast of lower power, a shorter pipeline and an additional feature termed ‘ Single Cycle execution’ for I/O

    • 32-bit, Low-Power Processor at an 8-bit Cost

      • The Cortex-M0+ processor has the smallest footprint and lowest power requirements of all the Cortex-M processors. The low-power processor is suitable for a wide variety of applications, including sensors and wearables.

    Slide 10 PPT 1: ARM Cortex-M0/M0+ & NVIC

    Slide 15 PPT 1: ARM Cortex-M0/M0+ & NVIC
    7.2 Cortex-
    • M0
    • Cortex-M0 was released in the year 2009.
    • It was the smaller ARM processor with highly energy efficient
    • It was good enough in performance for most embedded applications
    • Based on its market review and performance, it was stated as ‘Fastest ever licenced ARM processor’.
    Slide 16 PPT 1: ARM Cortex-M0/M0+ & NVIC
    It mainly focus on low power and small silicon area while keeping the performance high.
    • For this 32-bit MCU, the power silicon area are almost the same as that of 8 or 16 bit MCU.
    • Its performance is 2 to 10 times better than many 8 and 16 bit processors.
    Slide 17 PPT 1: ARM Cortex-M0/M0+ & NVIC
    Three-stage pipeline for Cortex-M0
    • The three stage pipeline of M0 is a regular fetch-decode-execute pipeline
    • It takes 3 cycles to process an instruction
    Slide 18 PPT 1: ARM Cortex-M0/M0+ & NVIC
    Wat about Cortex-M0+?
    • Cortex-M0+ was released three years later.
    • Performance wise, the core is an upgraded version of M0
    • Uses the same software tools.
    • But difference, M0 has 3-stage pipeline whereas M0+ has only 2-stages.
    • The smaller pipeline helps in more power savings.
    • Cortex-M0+ is ideal for academic purposes, boards development and also quite inexpensive.
    Slide 19 PPT 1: ARM Cortex-M0/M0+ & NVIC
    The architectural features of all members of the Cortex-M series have many things in common
    • So, need to understand thorough concept of Cortex-Mo is the first step.
    • So we discuss here only on M0 architecture keeping in mind that the M0+ is almost same.
    • This makes it easier to understand other members of series.
    Slide 20 PPT 1: ARM Cortex-M0/M0+ & NVIC
    Two-stage pipeline for Cortex-M0+
    • M0+ has 2 stages
    • fetch, decode & execute are completed in two cycles
    Slide 21 PPT 1: ARM Cortex-M0/M0+ & NVIC
    Advantages for a designer to choose M0 for his design
    • Its architecture is simple and easy to learn and understand
    • It has low gate count
    • Its performance is quite good as far as comparison capability is concerned
    • It operates at very low power levels and is energy efficient
    • Its code density is high
    • Its interrupt handling is deterministic
    • It is upward compatible with the whole Cortex- M family
    • It has an integrated Memory Protection Unit (Optional) and thus is capable of providing platform security.
    • Its design is optimised for low power and low area
    Slide 22 PPT 1: ARM Cortex-M0/M0+ & NVIC
    Features of Cortex-M0
    • It is a 32-Bit processor using ARM v6M ISA.
    • Most of its instructions have single cycle execution capability
    • It has only 56 base instructions, through some instructions have more than one form of usage
    • It is a three-stage pipeline design
    • It is a Von
    • Neuman
    • design

    • Once the specified interrupt is acknowledged, the interrupting device can access its interrupt handler
    • The NVIC has been designed for interrupt latency to be low and deterministic.

    • controller

    • Thereafter, interrupt handling is done by NVIC

    7.3.2.1 Thread Mode
    • When
    • the processor is powered on, it is in the Thread Mode

    • There are two stacks defined for the processor:
    • 1. Main Stack
    • 2. Process Stack

      • In the Thread mode, either of them may be used and a control register can be configured to decide whether the processor should use the main stack or the process stack.

    • Figure shows the registers of the core

    Slide 37 PPT 1: ARM Cortex-M0/M0+ & NVIC

    • 1. Stack pointer (R13)
    • Processor uses a descending stack
    • Stack pointer which is also called R13 consists of 2 entities:
    • MSP (Main Stack Pointer)
    • PSP (Processor Stack Pointer)
    • MSP is a default stack pointer, it is also the stack pointer used in handing interrupt handlers
    • Few applications need only one stack pointer, so MSP will be used
    • System with OS takes use of MSP for OS tasks and PSP for the use of applications
    Slide 38 PPT 1: ARM Cortex-M0/M0+ & NVIC
    7.4.2 special registers
    • 2. L
    • ink register (R14)
    • As soon as function is called or an interrupt occurs, control branches to a new location
    • Address to which to return to, is the current value of PC, is saved
    • Register in which the return address is stored is the Link Register
    • At the end of function, the contents of the Link Register can be copied back to PC

      • Such a register increases speed because when functions are called, the return address is available in a processor register, rather to a stack.

    • Advantage is because registers are within CPU, but memory is outside it and memory access is slower.
    Slide 39 PPT 1: ARM Cortex-M0/M0+ & NVIC

    • Means there are ways by which can cause branching by simply writing an address into the program counter

    • Clearing
    • this bit causes an exception
