    7.5.1 Types and Attributes of memory
    • Have seen the different sections of the memory space allocated for different applications
    • Access to different areas of memory may be ‘Shareable’ if there is more than one processor/bus master in the system
    • The order in which access is allowed to occur for region of memory, is defined by certain memory attributes
    • they are:
    • Normal, Device and Strongly-ordered
    Slide 54 PPT 1: ARM Cortex-M0/M0+ & NVIC
    1. Normal
    • Memory used for program execution and data storage generally occurs within the ‘Normal’ designation.
    • In such kind, the processor has the freedom to do ‘Out of Order’ accessing and even speculative reads
    • Means that memory access need not be in the order in which a program is coded
    • Examples: Preprogramed flash
    • ROM, SDRAM, SRAM and DDR memory
    Slide 55 PPT 1: ARM Cortex-M0/M0+ & NVIC
    2. Device
    • In the case of I/O’s rules of access is stricter
    • Out of order is not permitted
    Slide 56 PPT 1: ARM Cortex-M0/M0+ & NVIC
    3. Strongly ordered

    This attribute is required where it is necessary to ensure strict ordering of the access relative to what occurred in program order before the access and after it

  • This means that the processor preserves transaction order relative to all other transactions
  • Strongly-ordered memory always assumes the resource to be shareable
    • Slide 57 PPT 1: ARM Cortex-M0/M0+ & NVIC
    7.5.2 Out-of-Order Access
    • As
    • the program is coded, compiled and made ready to run, we assume that execution is in the order of the program lines.
    • Program may change the order of execution
    • It may also give higher efficiency and speed
    • Similarly, the order in which the memory is accessed may also be changed.
    Slide 58 PPT 1: ARM Cortex-M0/M0+ & NVIC
    Reasons
    • behind it is listed as:
    • The processor can reorder same memory
    • Memory or devices in the memory map may have different wait states
    • Some memory accesses are buffered or speculative
    Slide 59 PPT 1: ARM Cortex-M0/M0+ & NVIC
    7.6 Nested Vectored Interrupt Controller
    • When

      • a currently executing program is made to stop and a new program is taken up for execution, we say that an interrupt has occurred.

    • The word 'exception' is also associated with this event.
    • we will use the term interrupt for all classes of such events.
    • The word 'exception' will then refer to a sub class of interrupts.

      • situations under which the currently running program has to be temporarily suspended to give way to another sequence of instructions

    • .
    Slide 60 PPT 1: ARM Cortex-M0/M0+ & NVIC
    7.6 Nested Vectored Interrupt Controller
    • One
    • case is when a peripheral needs service from the processor
    • .
    • The
    • request from the peripheral may appear at any time with no relation to the clock of the processor.
    • Such
    • requests are 'asynchronous', that is, they appear at arbitrary instances and are not timed with the system clock
    • .
    • Such
    • asynchronous requests (for service) from peripherals are titled as IRQs
    • (Inter­rupt
    • Request
    • ).
    • There are interrupts which are generated by conditions caused by the processor — faults or certain special conditions.
    Slide 61 PPT 1: ARM Cortex-M0/M0+ & NVIC
    Shall
    • call them 'system exceptions' or just exceptions.
    • Some of these exceptions are 'synchronous' with respect to the CPU clock.
    • They are produced by the CPU control unit when faults or certain special conditions occur.
    • The control unit generates them only when the current instruction is terminated/completed.
    • For our Cortex-MO, '
    • SvCall
    • ' and 'hard fault' are synchronous exceptions
    • whereas all other exceptions are asynchronous.
    Slide 62 PPT 1: ARM Cortex-M0/M0+ & NVIC
    Figure illustrates the 'interrupt/ exception model' of the processor
    • .
    • Let
    • us now move onto understanding this model in greater depth.
    Slide 63 PPT 1: ARM Cortex-M0/M0+ & NVIC
    The
    • NVIC
    • in the Cortex-MO processor family provides very flexible and easy interrupt management
    • .
    • It

      • has features of programmable priority levels, automatic nested interrupt support, along with support for multiple inter­rupt masking

    • .
    • In

      • addition to interrupt requests from peripherals, the NVIC supports internal interrupts, for example, an input from the 24-bit timer call

    • SysTick
    • , which is often used by the OS.
    • There
    • are also additional system exceptions to support OS operations, and a non-
    • Maskable
    • Interrupt (NMI) input.
    Slide 64 PPT 1: ARM Cortex-M0/M0+ & NVIC
    Figure shows the NVIC in greater detail
    • Let us first talk about the
    • SysTimer
    • which is shown as a block in Fig.
    • The
    • SysTimer
    • is an optional block for Cortex-MO
    Slide 65 PPT 1: ARM Cortex-M0/M0+ & NVIC
    What are the uses of the
    • SysTimer
    • ?
    • It operates just like an ordinary timer.
    • There is a 24-bit register named
    • SysTick
    • Current Value Register (SYST_CVR).
    • The
    • SysTimer

      • is a down counter. It can be loaded with a number and when the timer is enabled, the value in this register decrements down to zero.

    • This event can be signaled by a Flag being set to 1 or an interrupt being generated.
    • After this the Flag can be made 0 and the count value re-loaded into the register and
    • the same
    • process of down counting and setting the Flag may be repeated.
    • SysTimer
    • timer
    • can be used as a reference for many applications
    • ex:
    • generating
    • square waves, PWMs, etc.

      • When there is an OS in the system, the counting down to zero can be signaled by an Interrupt and this interrupt will be generated every time

    • the
    • count reaches zero
    • .
    • This, then
    • becomes a regular
    • SysTick
    • or a time keeper
    • for the
    • system.
    Slide 66 PPT 1: ARM Cortex-M0/M0+ & NVIC
    7.6.1 Features of NVIC
    • The main features of the NVIC as described in the user manual
    • are
    • An implementation-defined number of interrupts, in the range 1_32.
    • A programmable priority level of 0-192 in steps of 64 for each interrupt.
    • Level and pulse detection of interrupt signals.
    • An external
    • NMI
    • The
    • first and second features talk about IRQs and say it is 'implementa­tion defined'.
    • This

      • means that the number of interrupt (IRQs) inputs and the number of priority levels are to be decided by the application require­ments, and defined by the licensees who do the final chip design

    • .
    • The
    • third point is about the ways in which a device may cause an interrupt to be triggered.
    • The
    • last point specifies that there is a non-
    • maskable
    • interrupt, which can be used by an external device
    • .
    • With
    • this basic understanding, let us attempt to make a thorough study of the Interrupt structure of Cortex-MO.
    Slide 67 PPT 1: ARM Cortex-M0/M0+ & NVIC
    7.6.1.1 Interrupt processing
    • Cortex-MO has an elaborate setup for handling interrupts.

      • The program to which control branches to, in the event of an interrupt, is generally called an ISR (Interrupt Service Routine) or Interrupt Handler.

    • Associated with each interrupt are three attributes:
    • Type number
    • Priority number
    • Vector
    Slide 68 PPT 1: ARM Cortex-M0/M0+ & NVIC
    Type
    • Number:
    • Each interrupt has a type number (also designated as exception number) which is fixed. The type numbers are used as
    • an index
    • to access the vector table.
    • Priority:
    • The priority of each interrupt is indicated by a number
    • a smaller number corresponds to a higher priority. Some specific
    • interrupts
    • are given negative numbers. Priorities are configurable
    • for
    • all interrupts except Reset,
    • HardFault
    • and NMI
    • .
    • Vector:

      • The term vector means 'address'. Each interrupt has an ISR. The address at which the ISR for each interrupt is stored is its 'vector'

    Slide 69 PPT 1: ARM Cortex-M0/M0+ & NVIC
    7.6.2 Interrupt vector table
    • When
    • an interrupt occurs, its vector is to be known by the system.
    • The
    • vec­tors of all the implemented interrupts are stored in a table, sequentially in an order specified as 'type numbers'.
    • Thus
    • , the first vector in the table is of Reset, which has a type number 1.
    • Each
    • entry in the vector table is an address and hence is a 32-bit number, that is, a word.
    • Each
    • interrupt vector takes 4 bytes of space and so vectors are found at addresses Ox0, 0x04, Ox08 etc.
    • This table is stored in the beginning of the memory map, that is, at loca­tion Ox000000000 onwards
    • .
    • This part of memory is implemented as ROM
    Slide 70 PPT 1: ARM Cortex-M0/M0+ & NVIC
    The first entry in the Table is the initial value of the stack pointer (MSP).
    • The size of the vector table depends on the number of interrupts implemented in the system.
    • If there are more peripherals, there will be more interrupting devices possible, and hence a bigger vector table.
    • Cortex M allows only 15 system exception and 321RQs.

      • The vector stored as an entry in the table is the address of the interrupt handler associated with that specific interrupt.

    • For example, see the entry corresponding to Interrupt 1 which is the reset interrupt, at address 0x04
    • The entry therein is the address to which control will branch to, when the processor is powered on or when reset
    Slide 71 PPT 1: ARM Cortex-M0/M0+ & NVIC
    How exactly do we get the handlers of various interrupts?
    • Consider
    • the case of the reset handler.
    • The system designer writes a start-up code and saves it in memory as a procedure.
    • The starting address of this code is loaded into the location 0x000004 of the vector table.
    • Similar is the case for the NMI handler, Hard Fault handler etc.
    Slide 72 PPT 1: ARM Cortex-M0/M0+ & NVIC
    7.6.3 Interrupts of Cortex-MO
    • Let us look into each of the interrupts in
    • detail
    • 1.
    • Reset:

      • This is a special interrupt. When the processor is powered up, control goes to the reset vector. This means that the first instruction to be executed will be taken from this address. Reset, if done any time during the course of the working of the processor, will again take program control back to the reset vector. It has the type number 1 and the highest priority level in the system, as indicated by the priority number

    • of 3.
    • 2.
    • NMI:
    • This stands for Non-
    • Maskable
    • Interrupt'. It can be associated with

      • a peripheral or triggered by software. In any case, it is meant for critical tasks. The NMI exception cannot be masked. It cannot be

    • pre-empted by interrupts other than reset. Its priority number is -2
    Slide 73 PPT 1: ARM Cortex-M0/M0+ & NVIC
    3.
    • Hard Fault:
    • This produced by the faults which occur during program running. It has a priority level of -1 and a type number of 3.
    • 4.
    • SVCall
    • :

      • This is a 'Supervisor Call ' Interrupt. It is a system exception triggered by the SVC instruction. It is useful in systems with an operat­ing system. Applications use the SVC instruction to get access to device driver functions and OS kernel functions. Its type number is 11, and its

    • priority
    • is configurable using the register SPRH2
    • .
    • 5.
    • PendSV
    • :
    • This is also related to OS-level services. In an OS environment, if no other interrupt is in the active state,
    • PendSV
    • is used for context switching. Its type number is 14, and its priority is configurable using register SPRH3.
    Slide 74 PPT 1: ARM Cortex-M0/M0+ & NVIC
    6.
    • SysTick
    • :
    • When the system timer count reaches zero, this exception is generated. This exception can be used for general counting
    • purposeses

      • and also as a system tick in an OS environment. It has a type number and its priority is configurable using the SPRH3 register

    • .
    • 7.
    • IRQs:

      • These are the interrupts generated by peripherals, but they can also be initiated by software requests. Peripherals always communicate with the system through IRQs. The number of IRQs implemented is dependent on the chip designer.

    Slide 75 PPT 1: ARM Cortex-M0/M0+ & NVIC
    7.6.4 Interrupt response
    • At
    • any time, each interrupt will be in one of the following states:
    • 1
    • .
    • Inactive:
    • It does not need service currently.
    • 2.
    • Pending:
    • It needs service and is waiting to be accepted.
    • 3.
    • Active:
    • This is the state of the interrupt which is currently being serviced.
    • 4.
    • Active and pending:
    • The interrupt is being serviced by the processor the same
    • and
    • there is a pending interrupt from the
    • saame
    • source
    • .
    • In all processors, interrupt handling is more or less similar
    • Once an interrupt is acknowledged, the most important thing to do is context saving.
    • The context is the content of the currently used registers.
    • The contents of these registers are pushed on to the stack.
    • If the processor is in the Thread mode, the stack that is being used could be the main stack or
    • the process
    • stack.
    • If the processor is in the Handler mode, it is the main stack that is relevant.
    Slide 76 PPT 1: ARM Cortex-M0/M0+ & NVIC
    Figure shows, eight words are saved on the stack.
    • These eight words are the contents of registers RO to R3, R12, LR,PC and PSR
    • This action of saving the context is called stacking and the saved content is called a stack frame.

      • Note that the stack frame includes the return address, which is the address of the next instruction in the interrupted program

    • This address would have been saved in the Link Register (LR
    • ) when
    • the interrupt occurred.
    Slide 77 PPT 1: ARM Cortex-M0/M0+ & NVIC
    7.6.5 Reset behavior
    • Reset is a special case of interrupt, because it corresponds to the starting of the processor activity.

      • See the sequence of actions that follow a reset (interrupt of type 1). Let's review the steps that follow this interrupt.

    • 1. The processor is reset (say, a Push Button reset)
    • 2. The 32-bit number in address Ox0 is loaded in the MSP.
    • 3.
    • The reset vector is taken from address 0x04.
    • 4.
    • The reset code is executed in Thread mode.
    • 5.
    • The application program is accessed after this.
    Slide 78 PPT 1: ARM Cortex-M0/M0+ & NVIC
    7.6.6 Issues in interrupt processing

    In this case, the pending interrupt with the lowest type number (essentially those that are more important) will be allowed precedence.

  • What if multiple interrupts have the same priority number?
  • Can an interrupt pre-empt a current handler?
  • The word
  • 'pre-empt
  • means
  • ‘remove
  • and
  • replace’.
  • The
  • answer
  • is
  • that
  • an interrupt
  • with a higher priority than
  • the currently active one may be allowed to do
  • that.
  • This
  • has to be decided as part of
  • the policy decided for the processor and/or
  • the operating system.
    • Slide 79 PPT 1: ARM Cortex-M0/M0+ & NVIC
    When an ISR is running, another interrupt can occur
    • .
    • If
    • all the interrupts are equal as far as importance is concerned, the second one can interrupt the first.
    • Third
    • one can interrupt the second one and so on.
    • This
    • is called nest­ing of interrupts'. This makes it a set of 'nested' interrupts
    • What is meant by a 'nesting of interrupts'?
    Slide 80 PPT 1: ARM Cortex-M0/M0+ & NVIC
    The

    NVIC of Cortex-M has the capability to handle nested interrupts in a way such that the interests of the system are safeguarded

  • .
  • The
  • points taken care of are
  • 1. High-priority
  • interrupts are serviced first and they cannot be interrupted
  • by
  • low-priority interrupts.
  • 2. A
  • high-priority interrupt may be allowed to pre-empt a low-priority interrupt handler.
  • 3. If
  • an ISR is not to be interrupted by any other interrupt, all other
  • interrupts
  • may be masked (except the NMI and Reset of course).
  • This
  • can
  • be
  • done
  • by setting a bit in the
  • PRIMASK register

    • 4. The NVIC supports dynamic re-prioritization of interrupts. This means that priority levels can be changed by software during run time. When an ISR is being run, if all other interrupts are disabled, the priority of any interrupt may be changed, without the risk of accidental re-entry.

  • H
  • ow
  • does the NVIC take care of interrupt nesting?
    • Slide 81 PPT 1: ARM Cortex-M0/M0+ & NVIC
    7.7 POWER MANAGEMENT USING SLEEP MODES
    • In general, any processor design takes care of power management
    • in various
    • ways.
    • One of the ways is by sleep modes in which minimum power is used.
    • When the processor sleeps, it does not do processing and can even go to a soft OFF.

      • Many embedded systems (like a mobile phone, for instance) need not work continuously, so a lot of power can be saved by putting it to sleep and then waking it up when needed

    • .
    Slide 82 PPT 1: ARM Cortex-M0/M0+ & NVIC
    Cortex-MO has two inbuilt sleep modes as listed below
    • 1. A sleep mode that stops the processor clock. This is the normal sleep mode.
    • 2. A deep sleep mode that stops the system clock and switches off the PLL and flash
    • memory

      • As a chip designer, he can use either one of them OR both of them ( for different levels of sleep) according to the needs of the applications.

    Slide 83 PPT 1: ARM Cortex-M0/M0+ & NVIC
    7.7.1 Entering Sleep Mode
    • Entering the sleep mode is done by executing specific instructions.
    • One instruction is the WFI (Wait for Interrupt) & other is WFE
    • (
    • Wait for Event)
    • In the case of WFI, as executed, the processor stops program execution and goes to the sleep state.

      • In the case of the execution of the WFE instruction, it checks the status of one-bit event register and acts accordingly as shown below for the bit value of 0 or 1.

    • if 0, The processor stops executing instructions and enters sleep mode.
    • if 1, the processor sets the registers to zero and continues executing instructions without entering sleep mode.
    Slide 84 PPT 1: ARM Cortex-M0/M0+ & NVIC
    The event register being found to be ‘1’ indicates that there is an external event which has to be taken care of and so ‘ Sleeping; is not the right thing at this point of time. Otherwise, it is okay to go to sleep.
    • Why is this so?
    • Sleep - on - exit
    • There
    • may be embedded systems that need to work only when an interrupt
    • comes
    • .
    • To

      • ensure that such systems go back to sleep immediately after exiting from an ISR, there is a condition named. SLEEPONEXIT

    • .

      • This is a bit in the SCR register and if it is set to '1', it ensures that after completing an ISR, sleep state is automatically entered

    Slide 85 PPT 1: ARM Cortex-M0/M0+ & NVIC
    The possible events are :
    • 1. Reset
    • 2. A debug event, if debug is enabled
    • 3. An exception or Interrupt with sufficient priority
    • How and When does the processor exit the sleep mode?

Low power dynamic sleep modes (Sleep Now, Sleep on Exit, Deep Sleep). Pipeline comparison: 3-stage pipeline (M0) vs 2-stage pipeline (M0+) with dynamic power optimizations.
